In this section we combine the results of optimization and data structure to prove Theorem
4.1
.

Lemma G.1
.

During the Main algorithm (Algorithm
17
), we have the following guarantees:

1.

Assumption
B.1
,
B.26
, and
F.5
about the error parameters are always satisfied.

2.

Assumption
B.5
that
\widetilde{\mu}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}\overline{\mu}
,
\widetilde{w}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}\overline{w}
, and
\overline{\mu}\approx_{0.1}t
is always satisfied.

3.

The
ClassicalStep
(line
25
of Algorithm
17
) is executed with probability at most
\frac{10}{n^{2}}
in each iteration.

4.

In expectation the repeat-loop on line
17
of Algorithm
17
is executed at most
2
times.

Notation

\epsilon

\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}

\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}

\lambda

b

Choice

10^{-7}/\log n

10^{-5}/\log n

10^{-7}/\log^{2}n

40\log n

10^{22}\sqrt{n}\log^{10}n

Table 15:
Extension of Table
7
. Summary of choice of
\epsilon
,
\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}
,
\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}
,
\lambda
and
b
. Assigned in
Main
procedure (Algorithm
17
). They are used to prove Theorem
G.3
.

Proof.

Part 1.
After plugging in the parameters in Table
15
, it is straightforward to see that the constraints stated in Assumption
B.1
,
B.26
, and
F.5
are all satisfied.

Part 2.
The assumption that
\widetilde{\mu}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}\overline{\mu}
follows from Part (b) of the correctness of
UpdateQuery
in Theorem
C.9
that
h^{\appr}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}h^{\new}
, and the assumption that
\widetilde{w}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}\overline{w}
follows from Part (a) of the correctness of
UpdateQuery
in Theorem
C.9
that
w^{\appr}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}w^{\new}
.

Finally, whenever
\Phi_{\lambda}(xs/t-1)>n^{3}
, the main algorithm runs the procedure
ClassicalStep
, and the
(x,s)
returned by
ClassicalStep
is guaranteed to satisfy
xs\approx_{0.01}t
(see
[
Vai89
]
). Also, if
\Phi_{\lambda}(xs/t-1)\leq n^{3}
, we have that
e^{\lambda|x_{i}s_{i}/t-1|}\leq n^{3}
, and since
\lambda\geq 30\log n
(Part 2 of Assumption
B.26
), we have
|x_{i}s_{i}/t-1|\leq 0.1
. Thus
\overline{\mu}\approx_{0.1}t
is always satisfied as well.

Part 3.
Let
\Phi^{(i)}=\Phi_{\lambda}(\frac{x^{(i)}s^{(i)}}{t^{(i)}}-1)
denote the value of the potential function in the
i
-th iteration. We use induction to prove
\E[\Phi^{(i)}]\leq 10n
, for all
i
. In the beginning of the main algorithm,
x_{i}s_{i}=1=t
,
\forall i\leq n
. Therefore in the base case,
\Phi^{(0)}=n<10n
. If the algorithm executes
ClassicalStep
in the
i
-th iteration,
ClassicalStep
outputs
x
and
s
that
xs\approx_{0.01}t
, and since
\lambda\leq 60\log n
(Part 7 of Assumption
B.26
),
\Phi^{(i)}\leq 10n
. If the algorithm doesn’t execute
ClassicalStep
, Lemma
B.27
gives us
\E[\Phi^{(i)}]\leq(1-\frac{\lambda\epsilon}{15\sqrt{n}})\E[\Phi^{(i-1)}]+\frac{\lambda\epsilon}{15\sqrt{n}}10n
. Therefore
\E[\Phi^{(i)}]\leq 10n
since we have
\E[\Phi^{(i-1)}]\leq 10n
from induction hypothesis.
Then using Markov’s inequality we have
\Pr[\Phi^{(k)}>n^{3}]\leq 10/n^{2}
. Thus the
ClassicalStep
on line
25
of Algorithm
17
is executed with probability at most
10/n^{2}
in each iteration.

Part 4.
From Part 4 of Lemma
B.16
we have that
\|x^{-1}\delta_{x}\|_{\infty}>3\epsilon
and
\|s^{-1}\delta_{s}\|_{\infty}>3\epsilon
each happens with probability at most
1/n^{4}
. Thus in expectation the repeat-loop on line
17
of Algorithm
17
is executed at most
2
times.
∎

Lemma G.2
.

For
\epsilon\in(0,1/10000)
,
\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}\in(0,1/10000)
, and
\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}=\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}/100\log n
, each iteration of
Main
(Algorithm
17
) takes

O^{*}\Big((\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot(n^{\omega-1/2}+n^{2-a/2}+n^{1+a-\widetilde{a}/2})+n^{(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a}+n^{1+b}\Big)

expected amortized time per iteration, where
\omega
is the exponent of matrix multiplication,
\alpha
is the dual exponent of matrix multiplication,
0\leq a\leq\alpha
and
0\leq\widetilde{a}\leq a\alpha
are the thresholds used by the data structure, and
n^{b}
is the sketching size.
O^{*}
notation hides all
n^{o(1)}
terms.

Proof.

Part 3 of Lemma
G.1
shows that in each iteration
ClassicalStep
is executed with probability at most
O(1/n^{2})
. Since the cost of
ClassicalStep
is
O(n^{2.5})
, the amortized cost of executing
ClassicalStep
is
O(n^{0.5})
for one iteration.

Part 4 of Lemma
G.1
shows that in expectation
OneStepCentralPath
is executed at most 2 times in each iteration. So now we only need to bound the running time of the procedure
OneStepCentralPath
(Algorithm
3
). In the procedure
OneStepCentralPath
, we call the procedure
UpdateQuery
of the data structures (Algorithm
8
) two times. Since the time analysis of these two data structure is the same, we are going to focus on one of them. Also note that the running time of
UpdateQuery
is the sum of that of
MatrixUpdate
,
PartialMatrixUpdate
,
VectorUpdate
,
PartialVectorUpdate
, and
Query
, so we analyze them one by one.

From what we proved in Lemma
B.28
and Lemma
B.29
, we have
C_{1}=C_{4}=\Theta(\epsilon)
and
C_{2}=C_{5}=\Theta(\epsilon^{2})
and
C_{3}=C_{6}=\Theta(\epsilon)
< 1/4.

By Theorem
C.9
and plugging in
\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}=\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}/100\log n
, the expected amortized cost per iteration of the following procedures are as follows:

\displaystyle\textsc{MatrixUpdate}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}((C_{1}\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}^{2}+C_{2}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}^{2})\cdot(n^{2-a/2}+n^{\omega-1/2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}((\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot(n^{2-a/2}+n^{\omega-1/2}))

\displaystyle\textsc{PartialMatrixUpdate}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}((C_{1}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}+C_{2}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}^{2})\cdot(n^{1+a-\widetilde{a}/2}+n^{1+(\omega-3/2)a}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}((\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot(n^{1+a-\widetilde{a}/2}+n^{1+(\omega-3/2)a}))

\displaystyle\textsc{VectorUpdate}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}((C_{4}\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{\mathrm{far}}}^{2}+C_{5}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{\mathrm{far}}}^{2})\cdot n^{1.5})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}((\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot n^{1.5})

\displaystyle\textsc{PartialVectorUpdate}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}((C_{4}\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{\mathrm{far}}}^{2}+C_{5}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{\mathrm{far}}}^{2})\cdot(n^{1.5}+n^{2a-\widetilde{a}/2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}((\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot(n^{1.5}+n^{2a-\widetilde{a}/2}))

\displaystyle\textsc{Query}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}({\cal T}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n^{\widetilde{a}},n^{a},n^{\widetilde{a}})+n^{1+b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}(n^{(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a}+n^{1+b}).

So the overall expected amortized cost of one iteration is

\displaystyle\penalty\ \textsc{MatrixUpdate}+\textsc{PartialMatrixUpdate}

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\textsc{VectorUpdate}+\textsc{PartialVectorUpdate}+\textsc{Query}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}\Big(\underbrace{(\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot(n^{2-a/2}+n^{\omega-1/2})}_{\textsc{MatrixUpdate}}+\underbrace{(\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot(n^{1+a-\widetilde{a}/2}+n^{1+(\omega-3/2)a})}_{\textsc{PartialMatrixUpdate}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ +\underbrace{(\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot n^{1.5}}_{\textsc{VectorUpdate}}+\underbrace{(\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot(n^{1.5}+n^{2a-\widetilde{a}/2})}_{\textsc{PartialVectorUpdate}}+\underbrace{n^{(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a}+n^{1+b}}_{\textsc{Query}}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}\Big((\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot(n^{\omega-1/2}+n^{2-a/2}+n^{1+a-\widetilde{a}/2})+n^{(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a}+n^{1+b}\Big),

where in the last step we use
\omega\geq 2
,
\widetilde{a}\leq a\leq 1
and
\omega-1/2=1+(\omega-3/2)\geq 1+(\omega-3/2)a
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove the main theorem of this paper.

Theorem G.3

(Restate Theorem
4.1
, Main result, third improvement)
.

Given a linear program
\min_{Ax=b,x\geq 0}c^{\top}x
with no redundant constraints. Assume that the polytope has diameter
R
in
\ell_{1}
norm, namely, for any
x\geq 0
with
Ax=b
, we have
\|x\|_{1}\leq R
.

Then, for any
\delta\in(0,1]
,
Main
(A,b,c,\delta)
(Algorithm
17
) outputs
x\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle c^{\top}x\leq\min_{Ax=b,x\geq 0}c^{\top}x+\delta\|c\|_{\infty}R,\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ and\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ }\|Ax-b\|_{1}\leq\delta\cdot(R\|A\|_{1}+\|b\|_{1})

in expected time

\displaystyle\penalty\ \widetilde{O}(n^{\omega+o(1)}+n^{2.5-a/2+o(1)}+n^{1.5+a-\widetilde{a}/2+o(1)}+n^{0.5+a+(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}})\cdot\log(n/\delta)

where
\omega
is the exponent of matrix multiplication,
\alpha
is the dual exponent of matrix multiplication, and
0<a\leq\alpha
.

In the ideal case when
\omega=2
and
\alpha=1
. The running time is
\widetilde{O}(n^{2+1/18})
. For general
2\leq\omega\leq 3
and
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
, the running time
is
O^{*}(n^{\omega}+n^{2.5-a/2}+n^{(8+\sqrt{19})/6})=O^{*}(n^{\omega}+n^{2.5-a/2}+n^{2.06})
.

Proof.

We use the parameters of Table
15
to prove the theorem. Since
t
is decreasing by a
(1-\frac{\epsilon}{3\sqrt{n}})
factor, the
Main
algorithm will take
O(\epsilon^{-1}{\sqrt{n}}\log(n/\delta))
iterations in total.

Thus the total running time is

\displaystyle\penalty\ \#\text{iterations}\cdot\text{cost\penalty\ per\penalty\ iteration}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O(\epsilon^{-1}{\sqrt{n}}\log(n/\delta))\cdot O^{*}\Big((\epsilon/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot(n^{\omega-1/2}+n^{2-a/2}+n^{1+a-\widetilde{a}/2})+n^{(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a}+n^{1+b}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O^{*}\Big(\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}^{-1}(n^{\omega}+n^{2.5-a/2}+n^{1.5+a-\widetilde{a}/2})+\epsilon^{-1}(n^{0.5+(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a}+n^{1.5+b})\Big)\cdot\log(n/\delta).

By plugging in the parameters
\epsilon=O(1/\log n)
,
\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}=O(1/\log n)
, and
b=\sqrt{n}\log^{10}n
(see Table
15
), the above running time becomes

\displaystyle O^{*}\Big(n^{\omega}+n^{2.5-a/2}+n^{1.5+a-\widetilde{a}/2}+n^{0.5+(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a}\Big)\cdot\log(n/\delta),

(69)

and recall that parameters
a
and
\widetilde{a}
need to satisfy that
a\leq\alpha
and
\widetilde{a}\leq\alpha a
.

Therefore, in the ideal case where
\omega=2
and
\alpha=1
, we can choose
a=\frac{8}{9}
and
\widetilde{a}=\frac{2}{3}
, and we have
2.5-a/2=1.5+a-\widetilde{a}/2=0.5+(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a=2+1/18
, so the above running time simplifies to

O^{*}(n^{2+1/18+o(1)})\cdot\log(n/\delta).

For general
\omega
and
\alpha
, the parameters are optimized as follows:

\displaystyle a=\begin{cases}\alpha,&\penalty\ \text{ if }\alpha\leq\frac{4w}{3(2w-1)},\\
\frac{4w}{3(2w-1)},&\penalty\ \text{ o.w. }\end{cases}

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}=\begin{cases}\min\{\alpha^{2},\frac{2}{2\omega-1}\},&\penalty\ \text{ if }\alpha\leq\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega-1)},\\
\frac{2}{2\omega-1},&\penalty\ \text{ o.w. }\end{cases}

Here we prove the final running time by discussing two cases.

1.

In the first case where
\alpha\leq\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega-1)}
, we have
a=\alpha
and
\widetilde{a}\leq\alpha^{2}=\alpha a
.

If
\widetilde{a}=\alpha^{2}
, then
1.5+\alpha-\widetilde{a}/2=1.5+\alpha-\alpha^{2}/2\leq 2.5-\alpha/2
since
\alpha\leq 1
.

If
\widetilde{a}=\frac{2}{2\omega-1}
, then
1.5+\alpha-\widetilde{a}/2=1.5+\alpha-1/(2\omega-1)\leq 2.5-\alpha/2
, since
\alpha\leq\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega-1)}
and
\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega-1)}
is the value that balances the two terms.
Thus the following inequality holds:

n^{1.5+a-\widetilde{a}/2}\leq n^{2.5-a/2}.

We also have
0.5+(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a\leq 1.5+a-\widetilde{a}/2,
since
\widetilde{a}\leq\frac{2}{2\omega-1}
and
\frac{2}{2\omega-1}
is the value that balances these two terms.
Thus the following inequality also holds:

n^{0.5+(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a}\leq n^{1.5+a-\widetilde{a}/2}.

Therefore the running time of Eq. (
69
) is dominated by
O(n^{\omega}+n^{2.5-\alpha/2})
in the first case.

2.

In the second case where
\alpha>\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega-1)}
, we have
a=\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega-1)}\leq\alpha
, and

\widetilde{a}=\frac{2}{2\omega-1}\leq(\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega-1)})^{2}\leq\alpha a,

where the second step follows since
\omega\geq 2
.
With these parameters, we have that

2.5-a/2=1.5+a-\widetilde{a}/2=0.5+(\omega-1)\widetilde{a}+a=\frac{13}{6}-\frac{1}{3(2\omega-1)}.

Therefore in the second case the running time of Eq. (
69
) is dominated by

O(n^{\omega}+n^{\frac{13}{6}-\frac{1}{3(2\omega-1)}})\leq O(n^{\omega}+n^{(8+\sqrt{19})/6}),

where
(8+\sqrt{19})/6\approx 2.0598\leq 2.06
is the solution of equation
\omega=\frac{13}{6}-\frac{1}{3(2\omega-1)}
.

Thus the running time in Eq. (
69
) is always upper bounded by

\displaystyle O^{*}\Big(n^{\omega}+n^{2.5-\alpha/2}+n^{(8+\sqrt{19})/6}\Big)\cdot\log(n/\delta).

∎

Appendix H
Multi-level with more details

In this section we provide more details for Section
2
.

H.1
LU-decomposition of Woodbury identity when
K=3

The LU-decomposition of matrix
D=\begin{bmatrix}M&U_{2}&U_{3}\\
V_{2}^{\top}&-C_{2}^{-1}&0\\
V_{3}^{\top}&0&-C_{3}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}
where the diagonal blocks of
L
are identity matrices is

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}I&0&0\\
V_{2}^{\top}M^{-1}&I&0\\
V_{3}^{\top}M^{-1}&0&I\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}M&U_{2}&U_{3}\\
0&-C_{2}^{-1}-V_{2}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{2}&-V_{2}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{3}\\
0&-V_{3}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{2}&-C_{3}^{-1}-V_{3}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{3}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}I&0&0\\
V_{2}^{\top}M^{-1}&I&0\\
V_{3}^{\top}M^{-1}&0&I\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}I&0&0\\
0&I&0\\
0&-V_{3}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{2}B^{-1}&I\end{bmatrix}}_{L}

\displaystyle\cdot

\displaystyle\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}M&U_{2}&U_{3}\\
0&B&-V_{2}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{3}\\
0&0&-C_{3}^{-1}-V_{3}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{3}-V_{3}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{2}B^{-1}V_{2}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{3}\end{bmatrix}}_{U},

(70)

where
B:=-C_{2}^{-1}-V_{2}^{\top}M^{-1}U_{2}
.

H.2
Online low-rank inverse and the oMV conjecture.

The following
static
data structure problem has received significant attention recently (see
[
HKNS15
,
BNS19
]

and references therein).

Definition H.1

(Online matrix-vector multiplication (oMV))
.

Preprocess a (fixed) matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
so that, given an online sequence of
T
vectors
h_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(arriving one by one), the data structure can efficiently output
Mh_{t}
(exactly) before the next iteration
t+1
.

The oMV Conjecture
[
HKNS15
]
states that with
\poly(n)
preprocessing time, the (amortized) query time
of any word-RAM data structure for oMV is at least
t_{q}>n^{2-o(1)}
. Note that this is in sharp contrast to
the
offline
setting where the vectors
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in T}
are given as a batch, in which case fast-matrix
multiplication can achieve
n^{\omega-1}<n^{1.37}
query time on average (assuming
T=n
, say).
Now consider the following static problem:

Definition H.2

(Online low-rank inverse multiplication)
.

Preprocess a fixed matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and a fixed vector
h
,
so that given an online sequence of
T
pairs of vectors
u_{t},v_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the data structure can efficiently output

(M+u_{t}v_{t}^{\top})^{-1}h
(exactly) before the next iteration
t+1
.

Perhaps surprisingly, we prove that these problems are essentially equivalent in the word-RAM model
with polynomial preprocessing time. We first show that oMV is at least as hard as the problem in Definition
H.2
:

Lemma H.3
.

If there is a data structure
A
with polynomial preprocessing time
for oMV (Definition
H.1
) with worst case query time
{\cal T}_{A}(n,T)
,
then there is a data structure
A^{\prime}
for the online low-rank inverse problem in Definition
H.2

with polynomial preprocessing time and
O({\cal T}_{A}(n,T))
query time.

Proof.

We design
A^{\prime}
as follows. In the preprocessing time, we use
O(n^{\omega})
time to pre-compute
the vector
x:=M^{-1}\cdot h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and run the oMV data structure
A
on the input matrix
M^{-1}
.
Recall in the query stage, we are given
u_{t},v_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By Woodbury’s identity, the solution

(M+u_{t}v_{t}^{\top})^{-1}\cdot h
can be written as

\displaystyle(M+u_{t}v_{t}^{\top})^{-1}h=M^{-1}h-M^{-1}u_{t}(1+v_{t}^{\top}M^{-1}u_{t})^{-1}v_{t}^{\top}M^{-1}\cdot h.

Thus, using a single invocation of the query algorithm of
A
, we can compute the product

y:=M^{-1}\cdot u_{t}
, and the remaining calculation is

\displaystyle M^{-1}h-M^{-1}u_{t}(1+v_{t}^{\top}M^{-1}u_{t})^{-1}v_{t}^{\top}M^{-1}\cdot h=x-y(1+v_{t}^{\top}\cdot y)^{-1}v_{t}^{\top}\cdot x,

which only involves vector inner product calculation and can be done in
O(n)
time.

Therefore,
A^{\prime}
takes
O({\cal T}_{A}(n,T))+O(Tn)
worst case query time. The lemma is proved by
observing that
{\cal T}_{A}(n,T)
is at least
\Omega(Tn)
.
∎

We proceed to the other direction of the proof.

Lemma H.4
.

Given a word-RAM data structure
B
with polynomial preprocessing time for
the online low-rank inverse problem, with worst-case query time
{\cal T}_{B}(n,T)
,
there is a data structure
B^{\prime}
for the oMV problem with polynomial preprocessing time and
O({\cal T}_{B}(n,T))
worst case query time.

Proof.

We construct
B^{\prime}
as follows: Given the input
M

in Definition
H.1
, the data structure first compute
M^{-1}
, and finds an arbitrary vector
h
for which
h^{\top}Mh\neq 0
,
then pre-computes
x:=Mh
and
y:=h^{\top}M
. This takes
O(n^{\omega})
preprocessing time. We can now invoke
the preprocessing function of the data structure
B
(for online low-rank inverse)
with inputs
M\leftarrow M^{-1}
,
h\leftarrow h
.
In each iteration
t\in[T]
, given the vector
h_{t}
of Definition
H.1
, invoke
B
with query vector
u_{t}\leftarrow h_{t}
,
v_{t}\leftarrow h
to get an answer
g
. Note that by Woodbury’s identity

\displaystyle g=(M^{-1}+u_{t}v_{t}^{\top})^{-1}h=Mh-Mu_{t}(1+v_{t}^{\top}Mu_{t})^{-1}v_{t}^{\top}M\cdot h=Mh-Mh_{t}(1+h^{\top}Mu_{t})^{-1}h^{\top}M\cdot h.

Then
B^{\prime}
outputs the query answer

\displaystyle(x-g)\cdot\frac{1+y\cdot h_{t}}{y\cdot h}=(Mh-g)\cdot\frac{1+(h^{\top}M)h_{t}}{h^{\top}M\cdot h}=Mh_{t},

which only involves vector inner product calculation and can be done in
O(n)
time.

Therefore,
B^{\prime}
takes polynomial preprocessing time and
O({\cal T}_{B}(n,T))+O(Tn)
worst case query time. The lemma is proved by
observing that
{\cal T}_{B}(n,T)
is at least
\Omega(Tn)
.
∎

As a corollary, we get that the following problem is at least as hard as the oMV problem:

Definition H.5

(Online cumulative low-rank inverse)
.

Preprocess a fixed matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and a fixed vector
h
, so that given an online sequence of
T
pairs of vectors
u_{t},v_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the data structure can efficiently output

(M+\sum_{i=1}^{t}u_{i}v_{i}^{\top})^{-1}h
(exactly) before the next iteration
t+1
.

H.3
Optimizing the parameters of Eq. (
3
)

Claim H.6
.

For any positive integer
n,K
, the following equation holds.

\displaystyle\min_{n=n_{1}\geq n_{2}\geq\cdots n_{K-1}\geq n_{K}\geq 1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}n\cdot n_{k}/\sqrt{n_{k+1}}+n_{K-1}\cdot n_{K}\right)=O(K)\cdot n^{1.5+\frac{1}{6\cdot(2^{K-1}-1)}}.

Proof.

Denote
p=\frac{1}{6\cdot(2^{K-1}-1)}
. Define
a_{k}
as the exponent of
n_{k}
such that
n_{k}=n^{a_{k}}
. Then it is equivalent to finding
a_{1},\cdots,a_{K}\in\mathbb{R}
such that the following three conditions hold:

1.

1=a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq a_{K-1}\geq a_{K}\geq 0
.

2.

1+a_{i}-a_{i+1}/2\leq 1.5+p
,
\forall i\in[K-1]
.

3.

a_{K-1}+a_{K}\leq 1.5+p
.

The optimal solution is given by
a_{i}=1-1/(3\cdot 2^{K-i})+(2-2^{-K+i+1})\cdot p
,
\forall i\in[K]
. Now we prove that the three conditions are satisfied with this solution.

Condition 1.

\displaystyle a_{1}=1-\frac{1}{3\cdot 2^{K-1}}+\frac{2-2^{-K+2}}{6\cdot(2^{K-1}-1)}=1-\frac{1}{3\cdot 2^{K-1}}+\frac{(2^{K-1}-1)/2^{K-1}}{3\cdot(2^{K-1}-1)}=1.

Since
a_{i}
is decreasing with
i
,
a_{i}\geq a_{i+1}
is also satisfied.
Finally,
a_{K}=\frac{2}{3}\geq 0
.

Condition 2.

For all
i\in[K-1]
,

\displaystyle 1+a_{i}-a_{i+1}/2=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1+(1-\frac{1}{3\cdot 2^{K-i}}+(2-2^{-K+i+1})\cdot p)-(1-\frac{1}{3\cdot 2^{K-i-1}}+(2-2^{-K+i+2})\cdot p)/2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1.5-\frac{1}{3\cdot 2^{K-i}}+(2-2^{-K+i+1})\cdot p+\frac{1}{3\cdot 2^{K-i}}-(1-2^{-K+i+1})\cdot p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1.5+p.

Condition 3.

a_{K-1}+a_{K}=(1-\frac{1}{6}+p)+\frac{2}{3}=1.5+p
.
∎

Appendix I
A feasible algorithm

In previous sections, we show a fast algorithm calculating an LP solution
\overline{x}
. However,
\overline{x}
is not always a feasible solution since we used sketching to calculate
\widehat{\delta}_{x}
and hence
A\widehat{\delta}_{x}
is not
0
. In this section we present how to turn the output
x
of Theorem
4.1
to a feasible LP solution, i.e.
\|Ax-b\|_{1}
is bounded. Our technique is based on the robust central path of
[
LSZ19
]
, and we extend it to two level update setting. Our algorithm use
G,M,u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}
to implicitly maintain the solutions
x=Gu_{1}+u_{2}
and
s=Mu_{3}+u_{4}
. Each iteration, the algorithm updates
G,M,u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}
so that
x
and
s
change by
\widetilde{\delta}_{x}
and
\widetilde{\delta}_{s}
in each iteration (see Definition
B.4
). In this way, we can postpone the expensive matrix vector multiplication to every
\sqrt{n}
iterations. The running time of maintaining
x
and
s
is dominated by the pre-existing computations, so our algorithm still achieves the same overall running time. Also since we do not multiply the sketching matrix on the left when computing
\widetilde{\delta}_{x}
and
\widetilde{\delta}_{s}
,
A\widetilde{\delta}_{x}=0
is always satisfied and in each iteration we always have
Ax=Ax^{(0)}=b
.

We introduce a smaller error constant
\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}
in this section.

Definition I.1
.

We define
\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}=\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}\cdot\epsilon}{3200\log^{3}n}
.

To make the output feasible, we present in this section the modified algorithms. In Section
I.1
we show that the error guarantees of central path method still has the same bound with the modifications, this section should be seen as a complement of Section
B
. In Section
I.2
we prove the correctness of this feasible algorithm when implicitly maintaining
x
and
s
, this section should be seen as a complement of Section
D
. In Section
I.4
we bound that the running time of maintaining
x
and
s
, this section should be seen as a complement of Section
E
.

I.1
Analysis

Consider the
j
-th iteration. Assume at the beginning of the
j
-th iteration, we have
x^{(j)}
,
s^{(j)}
,
\overline{x}^{(j)}
,
\overline{s}^{(j)}
. Define
w:=w^{(j)}=x^{(j)}s^{(j)}
,
\mu:=\mu^{(j)}=\frac{x^{(j)}}{s^{(j)}}
,
\overline{w}:=\overline{w}^{(j)}=\overline{x}^{(j)}\overline{s}^{(j)}
,
\overline{\mu}:=\overline{\mu}^{(j)}=\frac{\overline{x}^{(j)}}{\overline{s}^{(j)}}
,
w^{\new}:=w^{(j+1)}=x^{(j+1)}s^{(j+1)}
,
\mu^{\new}:=\mu^{(j+1)}=\frac{x^{(j+1)}}{s^{(j+1)}}
. We will prove
inductively
that the guarantees of Section
B
are still satisfied for the modified algorithm.

Robustness of central path.

We first prove the following two statements about the robustness of central path method:

1.

The
w^{\appr}
and
h^{\appr}
used in the data structures satisfy
w^{\appr}\approx_{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}w
, and
h^{\appr}\approx_{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}\mu
. (This corresponds to Part 1 and 2 of Assumption
B.5
. We only lose a constant factor here.)

2.

\mu^{\new}\approx_{0.2}t
. (This corresponds to Part 3 of Assumption
B.5
. We only loose a constant factor here.)

In Part 3 of Theorem
I.2
, we prove that in any iteration,
x,\overline{x}
,
s,\overline{s}
are entry-wise close with high probability, i.e.
\overline{x}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}x
and
\overline{s}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}s
holds with probability
1-1/\poly(n)
. We will use this to prove that the above two statements are still satisfied in our modified algorithm.

1.

The data structure directly ensures
w^{\appr}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}\overline{w}
, and
h^{\appr}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}\overline{\mu}
. And since
\overline{x}^{(j)}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}x^{(j)}
and
\overline{s}^{(j)}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}s^{(j)}
(Part 3 of Theorem
I.2
) we directly get the desired result that
w^{\appr}\approx_{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}w
and
h^{\appr}\approx_{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}\mu
.

2.

Note that previously
\mu\approx_{0.1}t
was proved in Lemma
B.27
by bounding the potential function. And the proof of Lemma
B.27
uses the bounds given by Lemma
B.21
. We define the potential function to be
\Phi(\frac{xs}{t}-1)
here instead of
\Phi(\frac{\overline{x}\overline{s}}{t}-1)
. Note that conditioned on
\overline{x}^{(j)}
and
\overline{s}^{(j)}
,
x^{(j+1)}
and
s^{(j+1)}
are deterministic. Thus Part 2 and Part 4 of Lemma
B.21
are trivial. We still have an analog of Part 1 and Part 3 of Lemma
B.21
:
\|\overline{\mu}^{-1}(\mu^{\new}-\overline{\mu}-\overline{\delta}_{t}-\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi})\|_{2}\leq O(\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}
and
\|\overline{\mu}^{-1}(\mu^{\new}-\overline{\mu})\|_{\infty}\leq O(\epsilon)
using the fact that
\overline{x}^{(j)}
and
\overline{s}^{(j)}
are close to
x^{(j)}
and
s^{(j)}
(Part 3 of Theorem
I.2
). Thus we still have an analog of Lemma
B.27
that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda}(\mu^{\new}/t^{\new}-1)\leq\Phi_{\lambda}(\overline{\mu}/t-1)-\frac{\lambda\epsilon}{15\sqrt{n}}\cdot(\Phi_{\lambda}(\overline{\mu}/t-1)-10n).

Using Part 3 of Theorem
I.2
again and the fact that the derivative of
\cosh(x)
function is constant when
x<2
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda}(\mu^{\new}/t^{\new}-1)\leq\Phi_{\lambda}(\mu/t-1)-\Omega(\frac{\lambda\epsilon}{\sqrt{n}})\cdot(\Phi_{\lambda}(\mu/t-1)-O(n^{2})).

Thus we can still inductively prove a polynomial upper bound on the potential function
\Phi_{\lambda}(\frac{xs}{t}-1)
. Note that we only loose a constant factor in the approximation ratio of
\mu
with
t
when the last term is
O(n^{2})
instead of
O(n)
.

w
and
h
move slowly.

The last part of the inductive analysis is to show that
w
and
\mu
are both moving slowly as that of Section
B.6
and
B.7
. Now we only have the guarantee for
w
and
\mu
, instead of
\overline{w}
and
\overline{\mu}
, so we use
\psi(w_{i}/v_{i}-1)
and
\psi(w_{i}/\widetilde{v}_{i}-1)
in the analysis and use
\psi(\overline{w}_{i}/v_{i}-1)
and
\psi(\overline{w}_{i}/\widetilde{v}_{i}-1)
for the algorithm (because the algorithm doesn’t know
w
and
\mu
). The amortized analysis of Section
F
need to be modified accordingly. We have the following two statements:

1.

The three bounds of Lemma
B.28
and Lemma
B.29
hold for
w^{\new}/w-1
and
\mu^{\new}/\mu-1
. (Originally used in subsection
F.4.3
,
F.3.3
.)

2.

Lemma
F.24
and Lemma
F.33
still hold for new potential function with use
w
instead of
\overline{w}
.

Proof Sketch.

1.

Note that Lemma
B.28
and Lemma
B.29
only use the relative error bounds stated in Lemma
B.16
. We can prove that all
\overline{x}^{-1}
,
\overline{s}^{-1}
and
\overline{\mu}^{-1}
terms of Lemma
B.16
can be replaced by
x
,
s
, and
\mu
since we proved that in iteration
j
,
x^{(j)}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\overline{x}^{(j)}
and
s^{(j)}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\overline{s}^{(j)}
. Following the same proof of Lemma
B.28
and Lemma
B.29
, and using the error bound of the
x
and
s
version of Lemma
B.16
, we can prove the desired statement.

2.

Since
x\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\overline{x}
in any iteration (Part 3 of Theorem
I.2
), we have
w^{(j+1)}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\overline{w}^{(j+1)}
, hence
\psi(\overline{w}^{(j+1)}_{i}/v^{(j+1)}_{i}-1)
is within the range of
[\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/v^{(j+1)}_{i}-1)-\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}},\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/v^{(j+1)}_{i}-1)+\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}]
. So it is fine to use
\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/v^{(j+1)}_{i}-1)
in the analysis while using
\psi(\overline{w}^{(j+1)}_{i}/v^{(j+1)}_{i}-1)
in the algorithm. The only problem we need to resolve is that when
v
and
\widetilde{v}
are updated to
w^{\appr}=\overline{w}
, the potential function is not cleared to be
0
, but instead remain a small number
\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}\ll\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}
. But this problem is already solved in Lemma
F.33
.

∎

I.2
Correctness of feasible algorithm

We first present the following main theorem, and prove it using lemmas proved subsequently.

Theorem I.2
.

In the
j
-th iteration of the while-loop from Line
19
to Line
44
of
Main
(Algorithm
18
), let
\overline{x}^{(j)}
and
\overline{s}^{(j)}
be the values of global variables
\overline{x}
and
\overline{s}
at the beginning of
j
-th iteration, and let
\overline{x}^{(j+1)}
,
\overline{s}^{(j+1)}
,
x^{(j+1)}
,
s^{(j+1)}
be the values of global variables
\overline{x}
,
\overline{s}
,
x
,
s
at the end of
j
-th iteration. Let
\widetilde{x}
,
\widetilde{s}
,
\widetilde{P}
,
\widetilde{\delta}_{t}
,
\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}
,
\widetilde{\delta}_{x}
,
\widetilde{\delta}_{s}
be defined as in Definition
B.4
. Let

be the subsampled randomized Hadamard matrix we used in our algorithm, defined in Definition
B.10
.

Then our algorithm guarantees that

1. The output of
OneStepCentralPath
(Algorithm
19
) satisfies

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}_{x}=\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}(I-(R^{\top}R)\widetilde{P})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \widehat{\delta}_{s}=\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{S}}{\widetilde{X}}}(R^{\top}R)\widetilde{P}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}),

\widehat{\delta}_{x}
and
\widehat{\delta}_{s}
match the definition in Definition
B.6
.

2.
In each iteration, when the algorithm reaches
MakeFeasible
(Algorithm
20
) on Line
28
of
Main
(Algorithm
18
), the following holds:

\displaystyle x-\big((\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{1})+(\mathrm{mp}_{t}.G)\cdot(\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{2})\big)-\big((\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{1})+(\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.G)\cdot(\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{2})\big)=x^{(0)}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}\widetilde{\delta}_{x}^{(i)}

\displaystyle s+\big((\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{3})+(\mathrm{mp}_{t}.M)\cdot(\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{4})\big)+\big((\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{3})+(\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.M)\cdot(\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{4})\big)=s^{(0)}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}\widetilde{\delta}_{s}^{(i)}.

3.
\overline{x}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}x
,
\overline{s}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}s
with high probability.

Proof.

For simplicity, we only prove these three statements for
x
. The case for
s
follows from similar reasons. Since we have two data structures sharing the same code, we denote
q_{x,t}
,
q_{x,\Phi}
,
p_{x,t}
,
p_{x,\Phi}
as the
q_{x}
and
p_{x}
defined on Line
17
and
18
of
UpdateQuery
(Algorithm
23
) in data structure
\mathrm{mp}_{t}
and
\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}
respectively. Also,
We denote
c_{t}
as the output of
ScalarC
(Algorithm
22
) of data structure
\mathrm{mp}_{t}
, and
c_{\Phi}
corresponds to
\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}
.
From the description of
ScalarC
, we have

\displaystyle c_{t}:=(\frac{t^{\new}}{t}-1)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ c_{\Phi}:=-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot t^{\new}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}\|\nabla\Phi_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mu}/t-1)\|_{2}}.

(71)

And recall
f_{t}
,
f_{\Phi}
defined in Line
13
, Algorithm
18
) are

\displaystyle f_{t}(x):=\sqrt{x}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ f_{\Phi}(x):=\nabla\Phi_{\lambda}(x)/\sqrt{x}.

(72)

In
UpdateQuery
(Algorithm
23
), the two data structure approximate
w^{\appr}
and
h^{\appr}
in the same way, so their
\widetilde{w}
(Line
10
) and
\widetilde{\mu}
(Line

and

) are the same. So they also have the same
\widetilde{x}
and
\widetilde{s}
(Line
16
). Note that
\widetilde{w}
,
\widetilde{\mu}
,
\widetilde{x}
,
\widetilde{s}
calculated in the data structure all matches Definition
B.4
.

We first show the following that will be used in both Part 1 and 2:

\displaystyle c_{t}\cdot f_{t}(\widetilde{\mu})+c_{\Phi}\cdot f_{\Phi}(\widetilde{\mu}/t)=

\displaystyle\penalty\ (\frac{t^{\new}}{t}-1)\cdot\sqrt{\widetilde{\mu}}-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot t^{\new}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}\|\nabla\Phi_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mu}/t-1)\|_{2}}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mu}/t)/\sqrt{\widetilde{\mu}/t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{\mu}}}\left((\frac{t^{\new}}{t}-1)\cdot\widetilde{\mu}-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot t^{\new}\cdot\frac{\nabla\Phi_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mu}/t)}{\|\nabla\Phi_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mu}/t-1)\|_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{\mu}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}),

(73)

where the first step is by the definition of
c
and
f
(Eq.(
71
),(
72
)), the third step is by the definition of
\widetilde{\delta}_{t}
and
\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}
(Definition

), the last step is by
\widetilde{\mu}=\widetilde{x}\widetilde{s}
.

Part 1.

First we calculate
q_{x,t}+q_{x,\Phi}
:

\displaystyle q_{x,t}+q_{x,\Phi}=\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}\left(c_{t}\cdot f_{t}(\widetilde{\mu})+c_{\Phi}\cdot f_{\Phi}(\widetilde{\mu}/t)\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi})

(74)

where the first step is by assignment of
q_{x}
(Line
17
of
UpdateQuery
, Algorithm
23
), the second step is by Eq.(
I.2
).

Next, we calculate
p_{x,t}+p_{x,\Phi}
. Note that the output
r
of

is calculated in the same way as before, so by Lemma
D.7
we have

\displaystyle r=R[l]^{\top}R[l]\sqrt{W^{\appr}}A^{\top}(AW^{\appr}A^{\top})^{-1}A\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})=R[l]^{\top}R[l]\widetilde{P}f(h^{\appr}).

(75)

Therefore,

\displaystyle p_{x,t}+p_{x,\Phi}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}\left(c_{t}\cdot r_{t}+c_{\Phi}\cdot r_{\Phi}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}\left(c_{t}\cdot R[l]^{\top}R[l]\widetilde{P}f_{t}(\widetilde{\mu})+c_{\Phi}\cdot R[l]^{\top}R[l]\widetilde{P}f_{\Phi}(\widetilde{\mu}/t)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}R[l]^{\top}R[l]\widetilde{P}(c_{t}\cdot f_{t}(\widetilde{\mu})+c_{\Phi}\cdot f_{\Phi}(\widetilde{\mu}/t))=\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}R[l]^{\top}R[l]\widetilde{P}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}\cdot(\widetilde{\delta_{t}}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}),

where the first step is by assignment of
p_{x}
(Line
18
of
UpdateQuery
, Algorithm
23
), the second step is by Eq.(
75
), the last step is by Eq.(
I.2
).

Then as we calculate
\widehat{\delta}_{x}
in line
8
of
OneStepCentralPath
(Algorithm
19
),

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta}_{x}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ q_{t,x}+q_{\Phi,x}-(p_{t,x}+p_{\Phi,x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi})-\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}R[l]^{\top}R[l]\widetilde{P}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}\cdot(\widetilde{\delta_{t}}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\widetilde{S}}}\left(I-R[l]^{\top}R[l]\widetilde{P}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}\cdot(\widetilde{\delta_{t}}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}).

Part 2.

We prove Part 2 by induction. In the basic case when
j=0
, we initialize
x\leftarrow x^{(0)}
, so it is true.

When
j>0
, let
j_{1}<j
be the last iteration that we call
Initialize
on Line
32
and
33
of
Main
(Algorithm
18
). In the
j_{1}
-th iteration, the
Main
algorithm executes Line
30
:
x\leftarrow x-(\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{1}+\mathrm{mp}_{t}.G\cdot\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{2})-(\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{1}+\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.G\cdot\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{2})

, and then re-initialize
\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{1}\leftarrow\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{2}\leftarrow\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{1}\leftarrow\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{2}\leftarrow 0
.
Therefore by induction on
j
, we have
x^{(j_{1})}=x^{(0)}+\sum_{i=1}^{j_{1}}\widetilde{\delta}_{x}^{(i)}
.

Now apply Part 3 of Lemma
I.3
, we have that

\displaystyle\big((\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{1})+(\mathrm{mp}_{t}.G)\cdot(\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{2})\big)+\big((\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{1})+(\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.G)\cdot(\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}c_{t}^{(i)}\cdot\sqrt{W^{\appr,(i)}}\widetilde{P}^{(i)}f_{t}(\widetilde{\mu}^{(i)})+\sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}c_{\Phi}^{(i)}\cdot\sqrt{W^{\appr,(i)}}\widetilde{P}^{(i)}f_{\Phi}(\widetilde{\mu}^{(i)}/t^{(i)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}^{(i)}}{\widetilde{S}^{(i)}}}\widetilde{P}^{(i)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(i)}\widetilde{S}^{(i)}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}^{(i)}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}^{(i)}).

where the first step is by Part 3 of Lemma
I.3
, the second step is by Eq.(
I.2
).

Also notice that in every iteration, we add
q_{t,x}+q_{\Phi,x}
to
x
in
OneStepCentralPath
(Line
10
of Algorithm
19
), so

\displaystyle x^{(j)}-x^{(j_{1})}=\sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}(q_{t,x}^{(i)}+q_{\Phi,x}^{(i)})=\sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}^{(i)}}{\widetilde{S}^{(i)}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(i)}\widetilde{S}^{(i)}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}^{(i)}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}^{(i)}).

where the last step is by Eq.(
74
).

Therefore,

\displaystyle x^{(j)}-\big((\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{1})+(\mathrm{mp}_{t}.G)\cdot(\mathrm{mp}_{t}.u_{2})\big)-\big((\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{1})+(\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.G)\cdot(\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}.u_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ x^{(j_{1})}+\sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}^{(i)}}{\widetilde{S}^{(i)}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(i)}\widetilde{S}^{(i)}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}^{(i)}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}^{(i)})-\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{X}^{(i)}}{\widetilde{S}^{(i)}}}\cdot\widetilde{P}^{(i)}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(i)}\widetilde{S}^{(i)}}}(\widetilde{\delta}_{t}^{(i)}+\widetilde{\delta}_{\Phi}^{(i)})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ x^{(j_{1})}+\sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}\widetilde{\delta}_{x}^{(i)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ x^{(0)}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}\widetilde{\delta}_{x}^{(i)}.

Part 3.

Consider a fixed coordinate
i
. We use
j_{i}
to denote the last iteration that the algorithm includes
i
into
\widehat{S}
when enter the
MakeFeasible
procedure on Line
28
of
Main
(Algorithm
18
) or when re-initialize. We prove the following properties in order to apply Lemma
I.5
.

1.

\overline{x}_{i}^{(j_{i})}=x_{i}^{(j_{i})}
. If the algorithm enter the
Initialize
procedure, we have
\overline{x}_{i}^{(j_{i})}=x_{i}^{(j_{i})}
. Otherwise we enter the
MakeFeasible
procedure, and according to the updating rule
\overline{x}_{\widehat{S}}\leftarrow x_{\widehat{S}}
(Line
4
of
MakeFeasible
, Algorithm
20
), we also have
\overline{x}_{i}^{(j_{i})}=x_{i}^{(j_{i})}
.

2.

t^{(j)}>t^{(j_{i})}/2
and
j-j_{i}\leq\sqrt{n}
, since the algorithm re-
Initialize
whenever it passes
\sqrt{n}
iterations or
t
changes too much (Line
29
in
Main
, Algorithm
18
).

3.

For all
l\in\{j_{i}+1,\cdots,j\}
,
w^{\appr,(l)}\in[w^{\old}/2,2w^{\old}]
, since the algorithm doesn’t include coordinate
i
in
\widetilde{S}
during iteration
l
.

Then by Lemma
I.5
,
x_{i}^{(j)}\approx_{\epsilon_{x}}\overline{x}_{i}^{(j)}
holds with probability at least
1-\delta
, where
\epsilon_{x}=\frac{200n^{1/4}}{\sqrt{b}}\cdot\log(n/\delta)\epsilon\leq\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}\cdot\epsilon}{3200\log^{3}n}
by our assignment of
b=10^{12}\sqrt{n}\log^{8}n/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}^{2}
(Line
7
of
Main
, Algorithm
18
) and
\delta=1/\poly(n)
. This satisfies the requirement of Def.
I.1
.

By choosing
\delta
to be
1/n^{10}
and union bound on all coordinates
i
,
x^{(j)}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}\overline{x}^{(j)}
holds w.h.p.
∎

The following lemma proves the invariants that are true throughout the algorithm. It is used to prove Theorem
I.2
. This lemma should be seen as a complement of Section
D
, and we directly use results proved in that section.

Lemma I.3

(Invariants)
.

In the end of the
j
-th iteration, the following invariants hold:

1.

G=\widetilde{V}A^{\top}(AVA^{\top})^{-1}A
.

2.

u_{3}+Mu_{4}=\sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}c^{(i)}\cdot A^{\top}(AW^{\appr,(i)}A^{\top})^{-1}A\sqrt{W^{\appr,(i)}}f(h^{\appr,(i)})
,

3.

u_{1}+Gu_{2}=\sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}c^{(i)}\cdot W^{\appr,(i)}A^{\top}(AW^{\appr,(i)}A^{\top})^{-1}A\sqrt{W^{\appr,(i)}}f(h^{\appr,(i)})
,

where
j_{1}
is the last iteration we call
Initialize
, and
c^{(j)}
is defined as in
ScalarC
:

in data structure
\mathrm{mp}_{t}
,

\displaystyle c:=

\displaystyle\penalty\ (\frac{t^{\new}}{t}-1),

and in data structure
\mathrm{mp}_{\Phi}
,

\displaystyle c:=

\displaystyle\penalty\ -\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot t^{\new}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}\|\nabla\Phi_{\lambda}(\widetilde{\mu}/t-1)\|_{2}}.

Proof.

We consider the
j
-th iteration in this proof.
Throughout the proof, we call the updated version of all date structure members at the end of the
j
-th iteration as the “new” version, with a “new” superscript in the notation, e.g.
u_{1}^{\new}
.

Part 1.
Note that
V
,
\widetilde{V}
and
G
are only updated in
MatrixUpdate
and
PartialMatrixUpdate
.

Case 1.
MatrixUpdate
(Algorithm
25
).
On Line
12
,
G
is updated to be
W^{\appr}M^{\mathrm{tmp}}
, where
W^{\appr}
is the new value of
\widetilde{V}
(Line
18
), and
M^{\mathrm{tmp}}
is the new value of
M
(Line
16
). We have

\displaystyle G^{\new}=\widetilde{V}M=\widetilde{V}A^{\top}(AVA^{\top})^{-1}A,

since in Lemma
D.17
we proved that the invariant of
M
still holds.

Case 2.
PartialMatrixUpdate
(Algorithm
26
).
First note that
V
and
M
are not updated in
PartialMatrixUpdate
. On Line
14
,
G
is updated to be
W^{\appr}M
, where
W^{\appr}
is the new value of
\widetilde{V}
, so the invariant of
G
still holds.

Part 2.

It suffices to prove that the additive term in the
j
-th iteration is

\displaystyle u_{3}^{\new}+M^{\new}u_{4}^{\new}-(u_{3}+Mu_{4})=c\cdot A^{\top}(AW^{\appr}A^{\top})^{-1}A\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr}).

Then starting from the
j_{1}
-th iteration where we re-
initialize
and set
u_{3}^{(j_{1})}=u_{4}^{(j_{1})}=0
, we can inductively prove the statement for iterations
i\in\{j_{1}+1,\cdots,j\}
.

There are three cases that we update
u_{3}
and
u_{4}
in the
j
-th iteration: in
MatrixUpdate
,
PartialMatrixUpdate
, or
Query
. Note that even though we might enter
Query
after executing
MatrixUpdate
or
PartialMatrixUpdate
,
u_{3}
and
u_{4}
won’t change inside
Query
since they were already updated in
MatrixUpdate
or
PartialMatrixUpdate
. So we can consider the updates to
u_{3}
and
u_{4}
in these three procedures separately.

Case 1,
MatrixUpdate
(Algorithm
25
).

\displaystyle u_{3}^{\new}+M^{\new}u_{4}^{\new}-(u_{3}+Mu_{4})=

\displaystyle\penalty\ u_{3}+Mu_{4}+c\cdot M^{\mathrm{tmp}}\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})+M^{\new}\cdot 0-(u_{3}+Mu_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ c\cdot M^{\mathrm{tmp}}\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ c\cdot A^{\top}(AW^{\appr}A^{\top})^{-1}A\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr}),

where the first step is by the assignment of
u_{3}^{\new}
(Line
14
),
u_{4}^{\new}
(Line
15
),
the last step is by
M^{\mathrm{tmp}}=A^{\top}(AW^{\appr}A^{\top})^{-1}A
that we already proved in Lemma
D.17
.

Case 2,
PartialMatrixUpdate
(Algorithm
26
).

\displaystyle u_{3}^{\new}+M^{\new}u_{4}^{\new}-(u_{3}+Mu_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ M(u_{4}^{\new}-u_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ M\cdot c\cdot\left(\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})-\mathbf{1}_{S^{\new}}B^{\mathrm{tmp}}\mathcal{L}_{r}[(M_{S^{\new}})^{\top}]\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ c\cdot\left(M-\mathcal{L}_{c}[M_{S^{\new}}]B^{\mathrm{tmp}}\mathcal{L}_{r}[(M_{S^{\new}})^{\top}]\right)\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ c\cdot\left(M-\mathcal{L}_{c}[M_{S^{\new}}]\cdot\mathcal{L}_{*}[\left((\Delta_{S^{\new},S^{\new}}^{\new})^{-1}+M_{S^{\new},S^{\new}}\right)^{-1}]\cdot\mathcal{L}_{r}[(M_{S^{\new}})^{\top}]\right)\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ c\cdot A^{\top}(AW^{\appr}A^{\top})^{-1}A\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr}),

where the first step is by
u_{3}^{\new}=u_{3}
and
M^{\new}=M
since they are not modified in
PartialMatrixUpdate
,
the second step is by the assignment of
u_{4}^{\new}
(Line
17
),
the fourth step is by the invariant on
B^{\mathrm{tmp}}
(Part
10
of Assumption
D.1
),
the fifth step is by Woodbury identity (Lemma
C.8
) and
M=A^{\top}(AVA^{\top})^{-1}A
.

Case 3,
Query
(Algorithm
24
).

\displaystyle u_{3}^{\new}+M^{\new}u_{4}^{\new}-(u_{3}+Mu_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ M(u_{4}^{\new}-u_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ M\cdot c\cdot\left(\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})+\mathbf{1}_{S^{\new}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{tmp}}-\gamma_{1}-\partial\gamma)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ c\cdot\left(M-\mathcal{L}_{c}[M_{S^{\new}}]\cdot\mathcal{L}_{*}[((\Delta^{\new}_{S^{\new},S^{\new}})+M_{S^{\new},S^{\new}})^{-1}]\cdot L_{r}[(M_{S^{\new}})^{\top}]\right)\cdot\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ c\cdot A^{\top}(AW^{\appr}A^{\top})^{-1}A\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr}),

where the first step is by
u_{3}^{\new}=u_{3}
and
M^{\new}=M
since they are not modified in
MatrixUpdate
,
the second step is by the assignment of
u_{4}^{\new}
(Line
25
),
the third step is by the close-form formula of
\gamma^{\mathrm{tmp}}-\gamma_{1}-\partial\gamma
(Eq. (
D.1
)),
the fourth step is by Woodbury identity (Lemma
C.8
) and
M=A^{\top}(AVA^{\top})^{-1}A
.

Part 3.

The proof for
u_{1}+Gu_{2}=\sum_{i=j_{1}+1}^{j}c^{(i)}\cdot W^{\appr,(i)}A^{\top}(AW^{\appr,(i)}A^{\top})^{-1}A\sqrt{W^{\appr,(i)}}f(h^{\appr,(i)})
follows from similar reasons as that of Part 2. We omit the details here.

∎

I.3
Bounding
x
and
\overline{x}

In this section we prove that the explicit
\overline{x}
is always within an error of
\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}
with the implicitly maintained
x
. This fact is used to prove Part 3 of Theorem
I.2
. Similar as in previous sections, we use a superscript
(j)
to denote the variable at the beginning of the
j
-th iteration.

Remark I.4
.

Our entire analysis is an induction-based argument. In the
j
-th iteration, the induction hypothesis allows us to assume that Assumption
B.5
is true for the
(j-1)
-th iteration, it also allows us to assume the following Lemma
I.5
is true for
x^{(j)}
and
\overline{x}^{(j)}
.

Using these two induction hypothesis we proved that Assumption
B.5
is still true for the
j
-th iteration in Section
I.1
.

Then we further use these two induction hypothesis together with
Assumption
B.5
for the
j
-th iteration
to prove the following Lemma
I.5
.

Lemma I.5

(
x
and
\overline{x}
are close)
.

Consider a fixed coordinate
i\in[n]
and a fixed iteration number
k\leq\sqrt{n}
. Let
b
denote the size of sketching matrix. If the following are true: (1)
x^{(0)}_{i}=\overline{x}^{(0)}_{i}
. (2)
t^{(k)}>t^{(0)}/2
. (3) There is a constant
w>0
such that for all
j\in[k]
,
w^{\appr,(j)}_{i}\in[w/2,2w]
. (4) Inductively Assumption
B.5
is true for iterations
1,2,\dots,k
.
Then we have:

\displaystyle|(x_{i}^{(k)})^{-1}(\overline{x}_{i}^{(k)}-x_{i}^{(k)})|\leq\epsilon_{x}

holds with probability
1-\delta
over the randomness of sketching matrices
R^{(1)},\cdots,R^{(k)}\in\mathbb{R}^{b\times n}
, where
\epsilon_{x}=\frac{200n^{1/4}}{\sqrt{b}}\cdot\log(n/\delta)\epsilon
.

Proof.

We denote
t=t^{(k)}
. Since
t^{(0)}\leq 2t^{(k)}
and the central path iteration will only decrease
t^{(j)}
, we have
t\leq t^{(j)}\leq 2t
for all
j\in[k]
.

From the definition of
\widetilde{\delta}_{x}
(Definition
B.4
) and
\widehat{\delta}_{x}
(Definition
B.6
), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}_{x}-\widehat{\delta}_{x}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{\widetilde{X}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}(I-\widetilde{P})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}-\frac{\widetilde{X}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}(I-R^{\top}R\widetilde{P})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \frac{\widetilde{X}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}(\widetilde{P}-R^{\top}R\widetilde{P})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}=\sqrt{W^{\appr}}(\widetilde{P}-R^{\top}R\widetilde{P})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}\widetilde{S}}}\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}.

(76)

Then the difference between
x^{(k)}_{i}
and
\overline{x}^{(k)}_{i}
can be written as

\displaystyle|x_{i}^{(k)}-\overline{x}_{i}^{(k)}|=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Big|\big(x^{(0)}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\widetilde{\delta}_{x,i}^{(j)}\big)-\big(\overline{x}^{(0)}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\widehat{\delta}_{x,i}^{(j)}\big)\Big|=\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\widetilde{\delta}_{x,i}^{(j)}-\widehat{\delta}_{x,i}^{(j)})\Big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \Big|\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sqrt{w^{\appr,(j)}}_{i}\big((\widetilde{P}^{(j)}-R^{\top(j)}R^{(j)}\widetilde{P}^{(j)})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(j)}\widetilde{S}^{(j)}}}\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}^{(j)}\big)_{i}\Big|,

(77)

where the second step is by
x^{(0)}_{i}=\overline{x}^{(0)}_{i}
, the third step is by Eq.(
I.3
).

We define a random vector
Y_{j}
for the
j
-th iteration:
Y_{j}=\sqrt{w^{\appr,(j)}}_{i}\Big((I-R^{(j)\top}R^{(j)})\widetilde{P}^{(j)}\frac{\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}^{(j)}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(j)}\widetilde{S}^{(j)}}}\Big)_{i}
.

We first bound the
\ell_{2}
norm of the right part
\frac{\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}^{(j)}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(j)}\widetilde{S}^{(j)}}}
:

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}^{(j)}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(j)}\widetilde{S}^{(j)}}}\Big\|_{2}\leq\Sup\Big[\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(j)}\widetilde{S}^{(j)}}}\Big]\cdot\|\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}^{(j)}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1.1}{\sqrt{t^{(j)}}}\cdot\|\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}^{(j)}\|_{2}\leq 2.2\epsilon\sqrt{t^{(j)}}\leq 5\epsilon\sqrt{t},

where the first step is by
\|a\cdot b\|_{2}\leq\Sup[a]\cdot\|b\|_{2}
, the second step is by the inductive Assumption
B.5
that
\widetilde{X}^{(j)}\widetilde{S}^{(j)}\approx_{0.1}t^{(j)}
, the third step is by
\|\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}^{(j)}\|_{2}\leq 2\epsilon t^{(j)}
(Fact
B.9
), the last step is by
t^{(j)}\leq 2t
.

By Lemma
B.14
, for each
j
and with randomness over
R^{(j)}
, and use the fact that
w^{\appr,(j)}_{i}\in[w/2,2w]
for all
j\in[k]
, we have

\displaystyle\E[Y_{j}]=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \E[(Y_{j})^{2}]\leq\frac{w^{\appr,(j)}_{i}}{b}\Big\|\frac{\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}^{(j)}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(j)}\widetilde{S}^{(j)}}}\Big\|_{2}^{2}\leq(2w)\cdot 25\epsilon^{2}t/b,

and with probability
1-\delta/n
,

\displaystyle|Y_{j}|\leq\sqrt{w^{\appr,(j)}_{i}}\cdot\Big\|\frac{\widetilde{\delta}_{\mu}^{(j)}}{\sqrt{\widetilde{X}^{(j)}\widetilde{S}^{(j)}}}\Big\|_{2}\cdot\frac{\log(n/\delta)}{\sqrt{b}}\leq(\sqrt{2w})\cdot 5\epsilon\sqrt{t}\frac{\log(n/\delta)}{\sqrt{b}}:=M.

Now, we apply Bernstein inequality on these zero-mean independent random variable
\{Y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
(Lemma
A.3
),
\forall\tau>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr\Big[|\sum_{j=1}^{k}(Y_{j})|>\tau\Big]\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{\tau^{2}/2}{\sum_{j=1}^{k}\E[(Y_{j})^{2}]+M\tau/3}\Big)

Choosing
\tau=64\frac{\sqrt{wkt}}{\sqrt{b}}\log(n/\delta)^{2}\epsilon
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\Big[|\sum_{j=1}^{k}(Y_{j})|>\tau\Big]\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{\tau^{2}/2}{50wk\epsilon^{2}t/b+\tau\cdot 5\epsilon\sqrt{2wt}\frac{\log(n/\delta)}{3\sqrt{b}}}\Big)\leq 2\exp(-10\log(n/\delta)).

Then take a union bound on all events that
|Y_{j}|\leq M
, we have
|\sum_{j=1}^{k}(Y_{j})|\leq\tau
with probability at least
1-\delta
. Therefore,

\displaystyle|x^{(k)}_{i}-\overline{x}^{(k)}_{i}|\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ |\sum_{j=1}^{k}(Y_{j})|\leq\tau\leq\frac{64\sqrt{wkt}}{\sqrt{b}}\cdot\log(n/\delta)^{2}\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\penalty\ x^{(k)}_{i}\cdot\frac{200\sqrt{k}}{\sqrt{b}}\cdot\log(n/\delta)^{2}\epsilon\leq x^{(k)}_{i}\cdot\frac{200n^{1/4}}{\sqrt{b}}\cdot\log(n/\delta)^{2}\epsilon\leq x^{(k)}_{i}\epsilon_{x},

where the first step is by Eq.(
I.3
), the fourth step is by
wt\leq 2w^{(\appr),(k)}_{i}\cdot 2t^{(k)}\leq(2x^{(k)}_{i}/s^{(k)}_{i})\cdot(3x^{(k)}_{i}s^{(k)}_{i})\leq 6(x^{(k)}_{i})^{2}
by Assumption
B.5
, the fifth step is by
k\leq\sqrt{n}
.
∎

I.4
Running time of feasible data structure

Lemma I.6
.

It takes
O(n)
time to execute
ScalarC
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward.
∎

Lemma I.7
.

In the procedure
MatrixUpdate
, it takes

1.

O(n^{2})
time to compute
G\leftarrow W^{\appr}M^{\mathrm{tmp}}

2.

O(n^{2})
time to compute
u_{1}\leftarrow u_{1}+Gu_{2}+c\cdot W^{\appr}M^{\mathrm{tmp}}\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})

3.

O(n^{2})
time to compute
u_{3}\leftarrow u_{3}+Mu_{4}+c\cdot M^{\mathrm{tmp}}\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})

Overall, refreshing
G
,
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
takes
O(n^{2})
time.

Proof.

This lemma directly follows from the algorithm of
MatrixUpdate
(ALgorithm
25
).
∎

Lemma I.8
.

In the procedure
PartialMatrixUpdate
, it takes

1.

O(n^{1+a})
time to compute
G\leftarrow G+(W^{\appr}-\widetilde{V})\cdot M
.

2.

O(n^{1+a})
time to compute
u_{1}\leftarrow u_{1}+(W^{\appr}-\widetilde{V})\cdot Mu_{2}
.

3.

O(n^{1+a})
time to compute
u_{2}\leftarrow u_{2}+c\left(\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})-\mathbf{1}_{S^{\new}}B^{\mathrm{tmp}}\mathcal{L}_{r}[(M_{S^{\new}})^{\top}]\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})\right)
. And computing
u_{4}
takes the same time.

Overall, refreshing
G
,
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
,
u_{4}
takes
O(n^{1+a})
time.

Proof.

From Lemma
E.1
, we have that when entering
PartialUpdate
,
\|w^{\appr}-\widetilde{v}\|_{0}\leq O(n^{a})
, and
|S^{\new}|\leq O(n^{a})
.

Part 1.
Since
(W^{\appr}-\widetilde{V})
is a
O(n^{a})
-sparse diagonal matrix, multiplying it with a
n\times n
matrix
M
takes
O(n^{1+a})
time. By memory operation, adding
(W^{\appr}-\widetilde{V})M
on
G
also takes
O(n^{1+a})
time.

Part 2.
Multiplying a
O(n^{a})
-sparse diagonal matrix
(W^{\appr}-\widetilde{V})
with a
n\times n
matrix
M
and then with a
n\times 1
vector
u_{2}
takes
O(n^{1+a})
time.

Part 3.
Computing
\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})
takes
O(n)
time. Multiplying a
O(n^{a})\times n
matrix
\mathcal{L}_{r}[(M_{S^{\new}})^{\top}]
with a
n\times 1
vector
\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})
takes
O(n^{1+a})
time. Multiplying a
O(n^{a})\times O(n^{a})
matrix
B^{\mathrm{tmp}}
with a
O(n^{a})\times 1
vector
\mathcal{L}_{r}[(M_{S^{\new}})^{\top}]\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})
takes
O(n^{2a})
time. Finally multiplying a
n\times O(n^{a})
matrix
\mathbf{1}_{S^{\new}}
that only has
O(n^{a})
non-zero entries with a
O(n^{a})\times 1
vector
B^{\mathrm{tmp}}(M_{S^{\new}})^{\top}\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})
takes
O(n)
time. Thus in total this step takes
O(n^{1+a})
time.
∎

Remark I.9
.

Note that this running time of
O(n^{1+a})
is always dominated by the
O({\cal T}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n,n^{a},\widetilde{k}))
time of other computations of
PartialMatrixUpdate
(see Lemma
E.18
).

Lemma I.10
.

In the procedure
Query
, it takes

1.

O(n^{a+\widetilde{a}})
time to compute
u_{1}\leftarrow u_{1}+c\cdot(W^{\appr}-\widetilde{V})\Big(\beta_{2}+M\cdot\big(\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})-\sqrt{V}f(g)+\mathbf{1}_{S^{\new}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{tmp}}-\gamma_{1}-\partial\gamma)\big)\Big)
.

2.

O(n)
time to compute
u_{2}\leftarrow u_{2}+c\cdot\left(\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})+\mathbf{1}_{S^{\new}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{tmp}}-\gamma_{1}-\partial\gamma)\right)
. And computing
u_{4}
takes the same time.

Overall, calculating
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
,
u_{4}
takes
O(n^{a+\widetilde{a}})
time.

Proof.

When entering
Query
, from Lemma
E.1
, we have that
\|w^{\appr}-v\|_{0}\leq n^{a}
and
\|h^{\appr}-g\|_{0}\leq n^{a}
, thus
\|\sqrt{W}^{\appr}f(h^{\appr})-\sqrt{V}f(g)\|_{0}\leq O(n^{a})
. From Lemma
E.1
, we also have that
\|w^{\appr}-\widetilde{v}\|_{0}\leq n^{\widetilde{a}}
, and
|S^{\new}|\leq O(n^{a})
.

Part 1.
We need to compute the following four parts:

1.

Multiplying a
n\times n
diagonal matrix
W^{\appr}-\widetilde{V}
with a
n\times 1
vector
\beta_{2}
takes
O(n)
time.

2.

Multiplying a
n^{\widetilde{a}}
-sparse
n\times n
diagonal matrix
W^{\appr}-\widetilde{V}
with a
n\times n
matrix
M
and then with a
O(n^{a})
-sparse
n\times 1
vector
(\sqrt{W^{\appr}}f(h^{\appr})-\sqrt{V}f(g))
takes
O(n^{a+\widetilde{a}})
time.

3.

Computing
\mathbf{1}_{S^{\new}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{tmp}}-\gamma_{1}-\partial\gamma)
takes
O(n)
time. Multiplying a
n^{\widetilde{a}}
-sparse
n\times n
diagonal matrix
W^{\appr}-\widetilde{V}
with a
n\times n
matrix
M
and then with a
O(n^{a})
-sparse
n\times 1
vector
\mathbf{1}_{S^{\new}}(\gamma^{\mathrm{tmp}}-\gamma_{1}-\partial\gamma)
takes
O(n^{a+\widetilde{a}})
time.

So overall this step takes
O(n^{a+\widetilde{a}})
time.

Part 2.
Computing
\sqrt{W^{\appr}}\cdot f(h^{\appr})
takes
O(n)
time. And multiplying a
n\times O(n^{a})
matrix
\mathbf{1}_{S^{\new}}
that only has
O(n^{a})
non-zero entries with a
O(n^{a})\times 1
vector
(\gamma^{\mathrm{tmp}}-\gamma_{1}-\partial\gamma)
takes
O(n)
time. Thus in total this step takes
O(n)
time.
∎

Remark I.11
.

Note that this running time of
O(n^{a+\widetilde{a}})
is the same as other computations of
Query
(see Lemma
E.3
).

Lemma I.12
.

The amortized running time of procedure
MakeFeasible
is

\displaystyle(C_{1}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}+C_{2}/\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot n^{1.5}.

Proof.

In this proof, we will use superscript notations that are consistent with section
F
.
Let
\overline{w}^{(j+1)}
denote the input
w^{\new}
of
UpdateQuery
in the
j
-th iteration.
Let
w^{\appr,(j+1)}
be the output of
UpdateQuery
in the
j
-th iteration.
Let
w^{\old,(j)}
be the values of
w^{\old}
at the beginning of the
j
-th iteration.
Let
\widehat{S}^{(j)}:=\{i:|w^{\old,(j)}_{i}-w^{\appr,(j+1)}_{i}|>w^{\old,(j)}_{i}/2\}
, and we define
\widehat{k}_{j}:=|\widehat{S}^{(j)}|
. Note that in the
j
-th iteration, procedure
MakeFeasible
takes worst-case
O(n\cdot\widehat{k}_{j})
time. We use a similar amortized argument as that of Lemma
F.19
.

We define the following potential function

\displaystyle\Phi_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(w_{i}^{(j)}/w_{i}^{\old,(j)}-1),

where the function
\psi
is defined as Definition
F.4
. We let
g\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the all one vector. Applying Lemma
F.23
(set
v^{(j)}
of the lemma statement to be
w^{\old,(j)}
), we have

\displaystyle(w\text{\penalty\ move})^{(j)}:=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\cdot\E\left[\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j)}_{i}-1)-\psi(w^{(j)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j)}_{i}-1)\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ w^{(j)},w^{\old,(j)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ O(C_{1}+C_{2}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot\|g\|_{2}=O((C_{1}+C_{2}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot\sqrt{n}).

(78)

From Section
I.1
, we have
w^{\appr,(j+1)}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}\overline{w}^{(j+1)}
. And from Lemma
I.5
we have
\overline{w}^{(j+1)}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}w^{(j+1)}
. So
w^{\appr,(j+1)}\approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}}w^{(j+1)}
.
For every
i\in\widehat{S}^{(j)}
, we have
|w^{\appr,(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j)}_{i}-1|>1/2
by definition of
\widehat{S}^{(j)}
, thus

\displaystyle|w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j)}_{i}-1|=

\displaystyle\penalty\ |(w^{\appr,(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j)}_{i})\cdot(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\appr,(j+1)}_{i})-1|

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\penalty\ 1/2-2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}-2\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}>2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}},

where the last step follows from
\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}<\frac{1}{10}
(Assumption
F.5
) and
\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}<\frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}{1000}
(Def.
I.1
). Thus
\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j)}_{i}-1)>\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}
from the definition of
\psi
. Since for
i\in\widehat{S}^{(j)}
,
w^{\old,(j+1)}_{i}
is updated to be
w^{\appr,(j+1)}_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle|w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j+1)}_{i}-1|=|w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\appr,(j+1)}_{i}-1|\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}+\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}<1.1\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}.

Thus
\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j+1)}_{i}-1)<0.6\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}
from the definition of
\psi
(Definition
F.4
).

So we have

\displaystyle(v\text{\penalty\ move})^{(j)}:=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\E\left[\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j)}_{i}-1)-\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j+1)}_{i}-1)\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ w^{(j)},w^{\old,(j)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i\in\widehat{S}^{(j)}}\E\left[\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j)}_{i}-1)-\psi(w^{(j+1)}_{i}/w^{\old,(j+1)}_{i}-1)\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ w^{(j)},w^{\old,(j)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sum_{i\in\widehat{S}^{(j)}}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}-0.6\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})=\Omega(\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}\widehat{k}_{j+1}).

(79)

Combining Eq. (
I.4
) and Eq. (
I.4
), we have

\displaystyle\E[\Phi_{T}]-\Phi_{0}=\sum_{j=0}^{T-1}((w\text{\penalty\ move})^{(j)}-(v\text{\penalty\ move})^{(j)})\leq T\cdot(C_{1}+C_{2}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot\sqrt{n}-\sum_{j=1}^{T}\Omega(\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}\widehat{k}_{j}).

Since when initialize we set
w^{\old}
to be
w_{0}
, we have
\Phi_{0}=0
. And since
\E[\Phi_{T}]\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{T}\widehat{k}_{j}\leq T\cdot(C_{1}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}+C_{2}/\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot\sqrt{n}.

Thus the amortized running time of
MakeFeasible
is

(C_{1}/\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}+C_{2}/\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{mp}})\cdot n^{1.5}.

∎

Remark I.13
.

Note that the amortized time of
MakeFeasible
is dominated by the amortized time of
MatrixUpdate
(Lemma
F.19
).

Table 17:
Let
\omega
denote the exponent of the current matrix multiplication. LP has
n
variables,
d=\Theta(n)
constraints, and all number can be encoded in
L
bits. The running time of all these algorithms has a nearly linear dependence on
L
. We consider the case where
A
is a dense full rank matrix.
\omega
denotes the exponent of matrix multiplication, and
\alpha
denotes the dual exponent of matrix multiplication. We remark that in some previous papers the running time is presented with explicit
d
and
\nnz(A)
.
Here we present the running time assuming
d=\Theta(n)
,
\rank(A)=n
and
\nnz(A)=n^{2}
.
